{mysection}

[]The choroidal microvasculature

The choroid is a highly vascularised structure of the eye located between the retina and the sclera
[
8
]
, and plays a crucial role in the physiology of the eye. It is one of the most vascularised tissues in the body
[
32
]
, citing the highest blood flow per unit tissue weight over any other
[
8
]
.

The uveal circulation branches from the ophthalmic artery and enters the eye external to the optic nerve, receiving 95% of the blood flow directed to the eye
[
9
]
, splitting into anterior and posterior segments. The anterior segment reaches the iris and ciliary body, while the posterior segment, or posterior uveal tract, supplies the choroid and accounts for more than 70% of the entire uveal blood flow
[
33
]
.

Embryologically, the eye first forms as an outpocketing of the forebrain, called the optic vesicle
[
34
]
. This undergoes invagination to form the retina and the
RPE
, each of which have their own dedicated circulations
[
9
]
, the latter being supplied by the choroid eventually. The choroid begins to form around six weeks of gestation as the choriocapillaris
[
9
]
, located posterior to the RPE. By eight weeks, the medial and lateral short posterior ciliary arteries (
PCA
s), which stem from the posterior uveal tract
[
35
]
, enter the choroid and branch into smaller arterioles toward the choriocapillaris
[
9
]
. The choriocapillaris are drained exclusively by vortex veins, in the absence of venous equivalents to the posterior ciliary arteries
[
36
]
.

Anatomically, the choroid is anchored posterior to the retina facilitating subretinal fluid exchange between the
RPE
and choriocapillaris and is connected laterally to the optic nerve. The choroid is attached anteriorly to the sclera by loose connective tissue which creates a potential space, the suprachoroid
[
9
]
. This potential space is traversed by vessels and nerves, and ranges in thickness from 10 to 35 microns
[
37
]
. However, this is often not visible using current imaging methods, with a thin suprachoroid reported to be visible in up to 44% of healthy people (74 eyes) aged 55 – 85 in
EDI-OCT

[
19
]
and a similar proportion (from 200 eyes) in
SS-OCT

[
38
]
. The inconsistency in the visibility of the suprachoroid in
OCT
when measuring the choroid can lead to poor agreement
[
19
,
38
]
, and so for the purposes of standardisation we consider the suprachoroid to be a separate entity to the choroidal vascular layer.

The choroid, bound between the suprachoroid and Bruch’s membrane, is structurally organised into three vascular layers commonly based on vessel size: the outer layer, known as Haller’s layer, consists of large blood vessels which are sourced from the posterior uveal tract. These larger vessels branch into smaller vessels in the middle segment, known as Sattler’s layer which then form capillary networks at Bruch’s membrane, known as the choriocapillaris
[
9
]
.

The choriocapillaris is a dense, anastomosed network of fenestrated vessels organised in a honeycomb-like, lobular pattern
[
39
]
, forming a thin sheet at Bruch’s membrane and is critical for the exchange of nutrients and waste with the
RPE
and outer retinal layers. The choriocapillaris layer has the highest capillary density in the body
[
40
]
and is most dense and thickest under the fovea, with an approximate depth of 10
\mu
m
[
8
]
. Individual capillaries are densely packed together with broad and flat lumens of approximately 20 – 50
\mu
m in diameter
[
39
]
. This means the choroidal capillaries have low vascular resistance, thus facilitating high choroidal blood flow
[
41
]
, to enable sufficient exchange with the
RPE

[
11
]
.

The choroid is the major source of oxygen to the photoreceptors, acting as the only source for the fovea — where inner retinal vessels are absent
[
8
]
. The nature of choroidal blood flow is exceptional, with a blood flow rate approximately 20 to 30 times greater than that of the retina
[
42
]
. This immense flow rate is more than sufficient — but not necessary — to meet the energy demands of the photoreceptors, which are heavily reliant on the choroid for oxygen and nutrient supply at all times
[
32
]
. Any significant reduction in choroidal blood flow can lead to rapid photoreceptor degeneration, contributing to conditions like Age-Related Macular Degeneration (
AMD
)
[
8
]
.

Moreover, The structural and functional integrity of the retinal and choroidal vasculatures is essential for normal retinal function
[
43
]
, and deficits in choroidal perfusion cannot be maintained by the retina and vice versa
[
43
]
. Unlike the highly permeable and freely anastomosing choriocapillaris, the choroid has a segmental blood distribution network
[
44
]
, delivering oxygen to localised, avascular zones of the retina
[
45
]
. This leaves strips known as watershed zones between the various
PCA
s, the short
PCA
s, the choroidal arteries, the arterioles, and the vortex veins
[
46
]
. This can lead to localised areas of filling defects and lower vascular perfusion which is thought to contribute to
AMD
or choroidal neovascularisation
[
44
]
.

Relative to the choroidal circulation, the retinal circulation is characterised by low flow and high oxygen extraction (approximate arteriovenous difference in partial pressure) of approximately 40%
[
42
]
. Conversely, choroidal blood flow has markedly higher flow rate with relatively low oxygen extraction of 3 – 4%
[
41
]
. The high blood flow is thus more than sufficient but not necessary to nourish the high metabolic demands of the photoreceptors
[
41
]
, which have the highest rate of oxygen use per unit tissue of any tissue in the body
[
47
]
. This suggests that alongside support for the photoreceptor metabolic activity and continuous outer segment renewal
[
32
]
, the excessive flow characteristics of the choroidal vasculature have other functions
[
41
]
, including potentially thermoregulation to cool the retina
[
33
]
and compensation for lens focusing during accommodation
[
8
]
. However, some believe the high blood flow is necessary to promote diffusion of oxygen and nutrients through the outer blood-retina barrier
[
9
,
32
]
.

The retinal vasculature is thought to have efficient autoregulatory functions
[
43
]
, an intrinsic property of many vascular tissues in the body which maintains perfusion pressure (constant blood flow) despite changes in systemic blood pressure and/or intracranial or intraocular pressure
[
48
]
. For the retina, blood flow is maintained across fluctuations in ocular perfusion pressure (the difference between mean arterial pressure and intra-ocular pressure) through various myogenic and metabolic functions
[
43
]
. This compensation constitutes to changes in retinal vessel calibre (vasodilation/vasoconstriction), which maintains the oxygen partial pressure at a low and normal physiological level
[
48
]
.

Interestingly, choroidal blood flow is not entirely autoregulated using traditional myogenic responses like the retina
[
8
]
. Instead, blood pressure management of choroidal vessels and response to retinal activity in the choriocapillaris are directly and indirectly under control by intrinsic choroidal neurons, respectively
[
32
]
. The choroid is well endowed with several choroidal nerve fibers (neurons), mostly situated at the central retina
[
8
]
. These neurons innervate (regulate) choroidal blood flow through vasodilatory (from parasympathetic and sensory nerve fibers) and vasoconstrictive influence (from sympathetic nerve fibers)
[
32
]
. Although, compensation for changes in ocular perfusion pressure is considered to be influenced by local, autoregulatory myogenic mechanisms
[
9
]
.

{mysubsection}

[]Systemic disease

Examination of the choroid has become increasingly popular with the technological advancements in non-invasive
OCT
imaging devices. Conventional
SD-OCT
led to the primary focus of
OCT
research on the cross-sectional retinal layers, with little attention paid to the choroid due to limited visibility. However, the advent of
EDI-OCT

[
12
]
and
SS-OCT

[
49
]
have significantly improved imaging of choroidal structures. Improved, non-invasive imaging of the choroid has in turn contributed to the nascent field of ‘oculomics’
[
50
]
, where the role of the choroid as a potential biomarker in numerous systemic diseases has been investigated
[
51
]
.

The choroid’s poor autoregulation results in choroidal fluctuations corresponding to changes in intra-ocular pressure, which itself fluctuates in response to systemic blood pressure due to diurnal variation
[
52
]
. The extent of diurnal variation in the choroid has been investigated previously
[
53
,
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
,
58
]
, reporting choroidal fluctuations of approximately 30
\mu
m over the course of the day, with the majority of research studies reporting larger choroid thickness measurements in the morning, compared to the afternoon and evening
[
52
]
.

Additionally, the choroid’s poor autoregulation makes it more susceptible to systemic changes in blood pressure. This has helped motivate investigations into conditions affecting systemic blood pressure, such as hypertension
[
59
]
and chronic kidney disease
[
60
]
. Additionally, the eye and the kidney show a striking resemblance in anatomy, physiology and response to disease
[
61
]
, and in particular the choroidal microcirculation seemingly reflects the renal microcirculation
[
62
]
. The endothelial cells of the choriocapillaris and renal cortex have similarly sized fenestrated vessel walls allowing subretinal fluid exchange in the choroid and blood filtration in the kidneys
[
62
]
. Moreover, the circulatory systems in the choroid and renal cortex have similar proportions of blood flow in contrast to their retinal and renal medullary counterparts
[
8
]
, with choroidal blood flow four-fold higher per unit mass than the kidney
[
62
]
.

Consequently, there has been interest and recent evidence of the choroid reflecting renal function. Choroidal changes have been linked to chronic kidney disease (
CKD
), with patients often exhibiting choroidal thinning correlating with severity of kidney dysfunction
[
17
,
63
]
. This relationship highlights the potential of choroidal imaging as a non-invasive method to monitor microvascular injury in
CKD
, as shown by Balmforth, et al.
[
60
]
, who suggested that an overactive sympathetic nervous system and systemic endothelial dysfunction were potential causes to choroidal thinning in pre-dialysis
CKD
. Additionally, in patients undergoing haemodialysis, the choroid can show significant fluctuations in thickness
[
64
,
65
]
, possibly due to changes in fluid balance
[
66
]
, offering insights into the impact of dialysis on systemic and ocular health.

Emerging evidence suggests potential links between the eye and neurodegeneration, such as in Alzheimer’s disease
[
67
]
, Parkinson’s disease
[
68
]
, multiple sclerosis
[
69
]
and cerebral small vessel disease
[
70
]
. For instance, Alzheimer’s disease patients or those with mild cognitive impairment often exhibit thinning of the choroid
[
15
,
71
]
, which could be linked to hypoperfusion and atrophy. However, the research community acknowledges the need for more methods to measure the choroid
[
24
,
69
]
, as paradoxical results have also been found in similar research contexts
[
72
,
73
]
. Although, the choroid’s response in the early prodromal stages of Alzheimer’s disease has not yet been elucidated, and this will be discussed in section
Choroidal Image Analysis for OCT Image Sequences with Applications in Systemic Health
.

Relative to invasive imaging of the cerebrovascular network
[
74
]
, which is limited by resolution
[
75
]
, non-invasive
OCT
imaging of the intra-ocular structures enables consistent and reproducible sampling of a microvascular site which is part of the central nervous system and may mirror cerebral hypoperfusion and microvascular dysfunction
[
76
]
. This has contributed to retinal biomarker investigation in systemic inflammatory conditions like septic shock
[
76
,
77
]
, which affects microvascular flow and permeability. Recent pre-clinical evidence
[
78
]
in a septic rat model reported reduced choroidal blood flow during shock, and other related studies have demonstrated feasibility of retinal imaging and measurement in
OCT
and
OCT
-Angiography (
OCT-A
) in a critical care environment
[
79
,
80
]
.

The choroid’s utility in systemic disease will continue to grow with advancements in the technology and accessibility of
OCT

[
81
,
82
]
. Ultimately, with sufficient imaging technology and standardised analysis tools, the choroid has the potential to provide a valuable window into systemic microvascular health and the health of vital organs like the kidney and the brain.

{mysection}

[]Optical coherence tomography

Figure 2
:
Typical horizontal-line, OCT scan from a Heidelberg Engineering OCT imaging device. En face localiser SLO image (A) and corresponding OCT B-scan (B), with annotations overlaid of major anatomical landmarks.

Previous methods for imaging/measuring the choroid, which sits posterior to the hyperreflective RPE, have used reflectance, sound and fluorescence. Light reflectance based on laser Doppler flowmetry
[
83
]
suffers from it’s limited representation, through only measuring localised choroidal blood flow at the choriocapillaris, typically underneath the fovea
[
9
]
, which is not representative across the posterior pole
[
45
,
84
]
. Ultrasonography use sound waves and vibrations to detect ocular tissue based on sound reflectance
[
85
]
, but typically have very low resolution and poor localisation which can result in less precise choroidal measurements
[
9
]
.

Indocyanine green angiography is more accurate for en face choroidal perfusion measurements in the early angiographic sequence (dye fill time)
[
86
]
, fluorescing over reasonably long wavelengths which can penetrate the choroid (790 – 805 nm) compared to fluorescein angiography
[
9
]
. However, this method is limited by it’s invasive methodology
[
11
]
, light absorption and scattering by the pigment in the
RPE
and choroid, and blood flow in the choroid
[
9
]
. Other methods have been considered such as ultrasonic colour Doppler imaging (combining ultrasonography with Doppler flowmetry)
[
87
]
and laser interferometry (measuring perfusion based on the distance between the cornea and retina during the cardiac cycle)
[
88
]
. However, these methods also face the aforementioned challenges around localisation and reproducibility
[
11
]
.

It has been established that optical imaging techniques help improve the resolution of imaging the choroid
[
9
]
, and optical coherence tomography, or
OCT
, has become the gold standard for clinical evaluation and imaging of the chorioretinal structures
[
11
,
89
]
.
OCT
was first introduced in 1991
[
10
]
and uses low coherence interferometry to construct a three-dimensional map of the intra-ocular structures at the posterior pole.

The general principle is that a tightly focused light beam is scanned across the posterior pole and measurements on back-scattered light which reflects from ocular tissue at various depths can then be processed. Interferometry leverages the phase difference between different light beams to infer reflectivity of the ocular tissue, otherwise known as
interference
. This is done first in the axial direction (A-scan) at a single lateral position, and iterated over in the transverse direction to create a two-dimensional cross-sectional image of the chorioretinal structures (B-scan). Doing this across the macula along the frontal plane (the final perpendicular axis to the transverse and axial direction) will enable imaging of three-dimensional intra-ocular structures
[
89
]
.

Figure
2
shows a standard
OCT
B-scan capture in panel (B), with the corresponding
localiser
en face image in panel (A). Landmarks are annotated on both images, and in particular the green horizontal line in panel (A) corresponds to the cross-sectional location of the B-scan on the macula. The localiser image is an en face scan of the retinal vasculature which can vary depending on the
OCT
system. For most datasets used in this thesis, we adopt the Heidelberg Engineering
OCT
system which leverages confocal imaging methods to capture an infra-red scanning laser ophthalmoscopy (
SLO
) image. The primary purpose of this image is to fixate the
OCT
beam at the correct location in the posterior pole (the green horizontal line in figure
2
(A)).

{mysubsection}

[]Time-domain OCT

Figure 3
:
Schematic of how
TD-OCT
works to generate an
OCT
B-scan. Note that this B-scan is from an
SD-OCT
device and is purely demonstrative of how the system works in general.

Figure
3
shows a schematic of the conceptually simplest approach to
OCT
, time-domain
OCT
(
TD-OCT
).
TD-OCT
works using low coherence light, i.e. light waves which have a short coherence length and can only interfere with each other over very short distances
[
90
]
. This interference is leveraged in
OCT
to create precise depth measurement with high axial resolution.

In the
OCT
setup in figure
3
, a single light source (superluminescent diode) emits a beam of broadband light with a short coherence length which is split into identical beams along two separate arms using a beam splitter. One beam penetrates the ocular tissue along the sample arm and back-scatters features of depth and reflectivity to the beam splitter, while the other beam moves along the reference arm and reflects off a mirror which is moving in the axial direction.

Because we use low coherence light, each position of the moving mirror generates a reference beam which interferes with one point in the sample beam to produce a signal. This location along the sample beam is equal to the optical length of the reference arm and corresponds to an axial depth in the ocular tissue. It is this optical path length and resulting interference between the reference and sample beams which creates the axial position and reflectance (pixel intensity) for a single pixel in an
OCT
B-scan
[
91
]
.

Varying the reference arm in length, i.e. the axial position of the moving mirror, has the effect of generating a reflectivity depth profile for a specific transverse (lateral) position. This is known as an A-scan, and represents a whole column (in the axial direction) of an
OCT
B-scan. By scanning a reflectivity depth profile (A-scan) along the transverse axis for the same sample, a two-dimensional
OCT
B-scan is generated by stitching the corresponding A-scans together. Finally, by repeating this process along the sagittal direction (vertically), three-dimensional
OCT
volume data (sequential and parallel
OCT
B-scans) is collected of the intra-ocular structures at the posterior pole.

While
TD-OCT
is conceptually simple and easy to manufacture
[
9
]
, it has some limitations. To create a good quality image requires significant sampling time as the
OCT
beam goes through the full depth during the sampling of each axial location per lateral position. However, this is limited due to safety standards, thus lowering the overall signal-to-noise ratio (
SNR
)
[
9
]
. The slower acquisition speed (400 A-scans/second)
[
20
]
, light absorption from the
RPE
and limited axial resolution (
\approx
10
\mu
m)
[
92
]
prevent sufficient choroid visualisation.

{mysubsection}

[]Spectral-domain OCT

Figure 4
:
Comparison of
TD-OCT
and
SD-OCT
for a single
OCT
B-scan of the same eye and approximately the same anatomical location. Reprinted with permission from Christopher Mody
[
93
]
.

A significant advance in
OCT
technology was removing the need for a moving reference arm and being able to collect reflectivity depth profiles (A-scans) in a single pass by working in the Fourier domain. This is called spectral domain
OCT
(
SD-OCT
)
[
94
]
. Low coherence light is split along the reference and sample arms, but the reference arm is fixed in
SD-OCT
image acquisition (the mirror in figure
3
does not move anymore). For each A-scan, the reference and sample beams are combined at the beam-splitter to create an interference pattern, as is typical in
TD-OCT
. However, in
SD-OCT
this pattern is passed through a diffraction grating which disperses the signal into individual frequency components
[
95
]
(the detector in figure
3
is now a spectrometer with a diffraction grating). While
TD-OCT
encodes the interference pattern (and depth of ocular tissue) as a function of the reference arm distance,
SD-OCT
encodes the interference pattern (and corresponding depth) as a function of frequency (or wavelength).

This spectral data encodes depth and reflectivity information of the ocular tissue. In particular, each frequency (or wavelength) in the spectra corresponds to a particular depth of the ocular tissue back-scattered from the sample beam, and is associated with a specific strength (or power). A charge-coupled device (spectrometer) decodes this spectral data using a Fourier transform, resolving and reconstructing a depth reflectivity profile, which is the A-scan. Once A-scans have been collected along the transversal axis at multiple lateral positions, the B-scan can be generated to create a two-dimensional
OCT
B-scan. Figure
4
shows the difference in image quality and signal between the same eye using
TD-OCT
in panel (A) and using
SD-OCT
in panel (B) at the same anatomical location.

Advantageously,
SD-OCT
generates an A-scan in a single pass, rather than requiring multiple sampling points like in
TD-OCT
. Therefore,
SD-OCT
is much more efficient and has a significantly higher scanning rate between 20,000 – 52,000 A-scans/second with a higher axial resolution of around 5 – 7
\mu
m
[
96
]
. However, while
SD-OCT
utilise infra-red wavelengths up to 850nm for greater penetration of the retinal and subretinal space
[
9
]
, light scattering from the
RPE
and the vascular nature of the choroid prevent penetration to the Choroid-Sclera boundary often
[
12
]
. Thus, conventional
SD-OCT
struggles to visualise the choroid and Choroid-Sclera boundary well, as shown in figure
4
(B).

Additionally,
SD-OCT
suffers from the sensitivity ‘roll-off’
[
11
]
, where deeper ocular structures tend to have poorer
SNR
. This sensitivity roll-off is in part due to the sampling of the spectrometer once it detects the interferometric signal, after it’s been passed through the grating
[
12
]
. The sampling is non-linear in the axial direction (as each A-scan forms), resulting in lower resolution (and signal) with greater depth. Additionally, increasing depth is associated with increasing frequency in the spectral data, and the limitations of the spectrometer prevents sampling of the highest frequency responses, resulting in a loss of measured signal intensity with greater depth
[
12
]
.

{mysubsection}

[]Enhanced depth imaging OCT

Figure 5
:
Comparison of an
SD-OCT
B-scan without
EDI
mode (A) and with
EDI
mode (B). Beside each B-scan plots the approximate signal sensitivity as a function of axial distance (from sclera to vitreous).

Figure 6
:
Different
OCT
B-scans of the same eye using the Heidelberg Engineering Spectralis OCT1 module. (A)
EDI
mode with an
ART
of 100. (B)
EDI
mode with an
ART
of 25. Conventional SD-
OCT
, known as non-
EDI

OCT
in this thesis, with an
ART
of 9.

The axial position (ocular tissue depth) which has the highest sensitivity (or signal) is called the ‘zero-delay line’, and corresponds to the ocular tissue depth which equals the length of the reference arm
[
9
]
. The sensitivity, and thus signal, decreases as a function of distance away from this depth, as these represent higher frequencies which are harder to sample. In conventional
SD-OCT
the zero-delay line is at the vitreoretinal interface, which is sensible since the vitreous is almost transparent and requires high sensitivity for good imaging
[
9
]
. The trade-off is that this produces poor quality choroid visualisation, as exemplified in figure
4
(B).

To mitigate for the sensitivity roll-off at the depth of the choroid,
SD-OCT
can leverage the conjugate property of the Fourier transform and alter the position of this zero-delay line. These adaptations to conventional
SD-OCT
give rise to enhanced depth imaging (
EDI-OCT
)
[
12
]
.

A consequence of taking the Fourier transform of the interferometric signal is that it gives rise to the real and conjugate A-scan, which is referred to as the real and inverted A-scan
[
12
]
(or B-scan after stitching the A-scans along the transverse axis). In practice, the real B-scan is used with peak sensitivity at the vitreoretinal interface, which corresponds to a sensitivity profile which decreases with greater depth. If the camera head is positioned nearer to the eye, this shifts the zero-delay line toward the inner sclera. Here, the inverted B-scan has an inverted sensitivity profile to that of the real B-scan, such that signal strength
increases
with greater depth (toward the zero-delay line, i.e. inner sclera)
[
9
]
.

This has the effect of enhancing deeper structures such as the choroid, as shown in figure
14
. In panel (A), conventional
SD-OCT
captured a B-scan (using the ‘real’ A-scan signals) whose sensitivity profile is shown to the right, and decreases non-linearly with increasing depth through the eye. This, as well as the light scattering properties of the choroid, prevent sufficient visualisation. In panel (B) we show an
EDI-OCT
B-scan. For this acquisition, the camera head is moved closer, and the ‘inverted’ B-scan is used as the output, which shows an inverted sensitivity profile to the right.

Heidelberg Engineering imaging devices now have
EDI-OCT
as an explicit option during image capture, by selecting the ‘
EDI
’ button. In addition to
EDI
as an option, Heidelberg Engineering also have active eye tracking (TruTrack, Heidelberg Engineering, Heidelberg, Germany) of the pupil during image capture
[
12
]
. This can allow for multiple (up to 100) B-scans to be captured during the same acquisition and averaged over to reduce speckle noise, which is called Automatic Real Time (
ART
) averaging. Figure
6
shows an
OCT
B-scan of the same eye with decreasing image quality, where
EDI
mode with an
ART
of 100 provides the most enhanced visualisation of the choroidal vessels and Choroid-Sclera boundary.

{mysubsection}

[]Swept-source OCT

A further advancement in Fourier domain
OCT
was the introduction of swept-source
OCT
(
SS-OCT
), which utilise longer wavelengths for greater depth penetration. Additionally, through the use of a wavelength-swept laser and photodetector hardware
[
49
]
,
SS-OCT
is able to mitigate for the sensitivity roll-off from which
SD-OCT
suffers
[
11
]
. In figure
3
, the
OCT
beam source emits a sweeping light source across a range of wavelengths and the detector is now a simple photodiode like in
TD-OCT
, rather than a broadband light source and spectrometer as in
SD-OCT
.

In
SS-OCT
image capture, a tunable laser is swept over the interference signal between the reference and sample arms across a range of wavelengths and in an ordinal fashion. Through the use of a simple photodiode, the laser’s output has a much higher throughput, processing A-scan reflectivity depths much more efficiently
[
9
]
and without the need for a grating or spectrometer
[
97
]
. This resulted in improved acquisition speed of up to 4x when it was introduced in 2012, relative to commercial
SD-OCT
systems at the time
[
98
]
.

In today’s commercial market, Topcon’s commercial
SS-OCT
DRI Triton reports a a superior A-scan rate of 100,000 A-scans/second
[
99
]
over Heidelberg Engineering’s Spectralis OCT2 module’s 85,000 A-scans/second
[
100
]
. Nevertheless, some commercial
SD-OCT
systems are still rivalling
SS-OCT
systems, with Optopol’s commercially available NX Revo-130 reporting scan rates of 130,000 A-scans/second
[
101
]
. However, it is thought that
SS-OCT
systems have potential for enabling
OCT
imaging speeds beyond 400,000 A-scans/second and even reaching speeds up to 1,000,000 A-scans/second
[
91
]
.

While acquisition rate is important for overcoming barriers to patient focus and concentration, especially in the context of disease, it is not the only metric on which to evaluate
OCT
systems. In the particular application of choroidal image analysis, deep, subretinal tissue penetration and signal strength are key. Interestingly, there exists a direct trade-off between acquisition speed and signal strength
[
102
]
. Signal strength is positively associated with
integration time

[
98
]
, the length of time it takes to capture an A-scan, and is thus inversely associated with acquisition rate. However, modern systems can counteract higher acquisition rate (and thus low signal strength) using post-acquisition image processing methods such as B-scan averaging (
ART
)
[
12
]
.

SS-OCT
can provide greater depth into the ocular tissue due to the longer coherence length of the wavelength-swept laser, relative to the shorter coherence length of the broadband light source of
SD-OCT
systems. This has a direct impact on the sensitivity roll-off, which
SD-OCT
suffers from more so than
SS-OCT

[
9
]
. Additionally, the use of a wavelength-swept laser enables a broader range of wavelengths up to 1050nm (with an approximate bandwidth of 100nm
[
97
]
) which allow for both the vitreous and choroid to be imaged simultaneously, rather than having to prioritise one as in
SD-OCT
and
EDI-OCT

[
9
]
. Ultimately, this allows for greater axial depth penetration in scattering tissues relative to shorter wavelength systems such as
SD-OCT

[
98
]
.

Figure 7
:
SNR
(sensitivity) roll-off curves (against ocular tissue depth) for
SD-OCT
Spectralis
OCT
, Spectralis
OCT
2 and Spectralis
OCT
2 with
EDI
-mode, compared to a hypothetical
SS-OCT
device. Reprinted from Teussink, et al.
[
98
]
with permission from Heidelberg Engineering GmbH.

Figure 8
:
Comparison of visibility of the Choroid-Sclera boundary in a patient with central serous chorioretinopathy, acquired using the Topcon DRI Triton (A), Zeis Plex Elite 9000 (B) and Heidelberg Engineering
OCT
2 Module with
EDI
mode (C). Note that panels (B) and (C) use an
ART
of 100. Reprinted from Teussink, et al.
[
98
]
with permission from Heidelberg Engineering GmbH.

However, the longer coherence length of the swept-source laser negatively impacts axial resolution, which is partially compensated for by the higher bandwidth of 100nm (compared to
SD-OCT
systems which usually have a bandwidth of 60nm around the central 850nm wavelength). Nevertheless, this leads to commercial
SD-OCT
systems having better optical in-depth resolution (7
\mu
m for Heidelberg Engineering’s Spectralis standard
OCT
module
[
103
]
compared with 8
\mu
m for Topcon’s DRI Triton
[
99
]
). Additionally, although longer wavelengths penetrate farther, they also suffer from greater water absorption which can lead to light attenuation
[
98
]
, as the beam has to penetrate through the vitreous which is primarily water
[
9
]
. This ultimately has an impact on the signal strength of
SS-OCT
systems.

Signal strength is often measured as the sensitivity of the
OCT
system or
SNR
. Teussink, et al.
[
98
]
state that “
SNR
is determined by the signal of a specific sample, how much light reaches the sample, how much light is reflected, and how much this light is attenuated before reaching the detector”. In their work, they estimate that longer wavelengths of 1050nm (with a bandwidth of 100nm) only lead to a factor increase in
SNR
of approximately 1.7, relative to an 850nm wavelength
SD-OCT
system (with a bandwidth of 60nm). This was observed even with a hypothetically higher input power (at the maximum safety limits) of the wavelength-swept laser source (meaning that more light would be reaching the sample)
[
98
]
.

For choroidal image analysis in
OCT
image sequences, maximal
SNR
at greater depth is optimal. As the sensitivity roll-off is improved in
SS-OCT
capture relative to conventional
SD-OCT
, this system is more optimal for choroidal visualisation. However, for spectral domain
EDI-OCT
, this sensitivity roll-off curve is inverted (figure
14
). Thus, regardless of higher wavelength and lower sensitivity roll-off,
EDI-OCT
still obtains higher
SNR
values for deeper ocular structures
[
98
]
, such as the larger choroidal vessels nearer the Choroid-Sclera boundary. Figure
7
shows the sensitivity roll-off curves for the Heidelberg Engineering Spectralis OCT1, OCT2 and
EDI
-OCT2 systems, and comparison to a hypothetical
SS-OCT
device.

Figure
8
compares the visibility of the Choroid-Sclera boundary for a patient with an enlarged choroidal space due to pachychoroid disease (central serous chorioretinopathy). The same location was scanned using Topcon’s
SS-OCT
DRI Triton, Zeiss’
SS-OCT
Plex Elite 9000 and Heidelberg Engineering’s
SD-OCT
Spectralis
OCT
2 module with
EDI
mode activated. Note that for Zeiss and Heidelberg Engineering, these B-scans were captured with an
ART
of 100. While the Choroid-Sclera boundary is visible across all three devices, the
SD-OCT
capture in panel (C) has greater contrast of the choroidal space.

{mysection}

[]Segmenting the choroidal microvasculature

Figure 9
:
EDI-OCT
volume scan acquisition (A), with segmentation tasks and measurements sought for choroidal image analysis (B).

Figure
9
shows a diagram with the output from a typical
OCT
volume scan acquisition in panel (A), showing the
OCT
volume block with the sequential B-scans and corresponding localiser
SLO
image. In panel (B), we show the segmentation tasks necessary for choroidal measurement, region and vessel segmentation. We also show the typical measurements of interest that are commonly sought after in choroidal image analysis including, for a single B-scan, thickness and area of the choroidal space and choroidal vessel density. This can also be extrapolated for an
OCT
volume scan to allow full choroidal mapping across the macula, measuring local choroid thickness and vessel density at different spatial locations of the macula.

Measurement of the choroid in
OCT
images require segmentation of the choroidal space and vessels, which is generally a harder problem than retinal layer segmentation. This is primarily due to the hyperreflective Bruch’s complex which can influence the
OCT
beam’s optical signal as it penetrates deep to the choroid. This can result in poor Choroid-Sclera boundary visualisation. While
EDI-OCT
and
SS-OCT
improve choroid visualisation, natural speckle noise still plays a significant role in poor Choroid-Sclera boundary visualisation
[
98
]
. Furthermore, the Choroid-Sclera boundary is typically identified from locating the posterior-most vessels appearing as dark blobs suspended in brighter-appearing connective tissue
[
104
,
105
]
. However, this is not always the case and the homogeneous appearance of the sclera and choroidal interstitial space can often obscure the true boundary location.

The choroidal vessels are highly heterogeneous in terms of size, shape, and edge contrast which make them hard to discern exactly. The choroid is also often plagued by low
SNR
which corresponds to high speckle and low contrast between the interstitial and vascular compartments. A final challenge is the oblique presentation of the vessels from cross-sectional visualisations. The choroidal vascular network is a dense mesh of blood vessels which makes it challenging to visualise individual vessels at perfect, circular cross-sections. Instead, oblique viewpoints of the choroidal vessels are seen on each
OCT
B-scan. Additionally, certain A-scans of the
OCT
B-scan are corrupted by shadows cast by superficial retinal vessels which branch out of the optic nerve head lying along the inner retinal layers. During acquisition, the
OCT
beam may penetrate through the retinal vasculature lying along the inner surface of the retina and sitting perpendicular to the incident laser light. This darkens the appearance of the interstitial and vascular compartments, which can often confuse automatic segmentation approaches
[
106
]
.

These reasons all contribute to the challenging tasks of choroid segmentation. While manual segmentation of the choroidal space and vessels is often done using Fiji (ImageJ)
[
30
]
or on the imaging device, this is a complex and time-consuming task, and prohibitively so for choroid vessel segmentation. Moreover, manual choroid segmentation suffers from high intra- and inter-rater variability
[
107
,
108
,
109
]
. Ultimately, manual segmentation is subject to the expertise of the grader, which may vary depending on experience as different graders may have different definitions of the Choroid-Sclera boundary — especially if the suprachoroidal space is present
[
19
]
. The subjective nature of manual segmentation prevents standardisation of choroidal measurements in the research community.

{mysubsection}

[]Region segmentation

{mysubsubsection}

[]Manual measurement

The lack of open-source and easily accessible computational approaches to characterise the choroid in
OCT
often lead to the research community leveraging manual methods
[
14
,
19
,
60
,
107
]
, and this has also continued as a standard approach even in recent years
[
15
,
17
,
22
,
65
,
73
]
. Due to the time it would take a manual grader to segment the choroid manually across the macula, representative thickness measurements are taken, usually underneath the fovea, i.e. subfoveal choroid thickness (
SFCT
). However, these often suffer from wide variability among graders
[
107
,
108
,
109
]
— especially when detecting the Choroid-Sclera boundary — due to poor visualisation
[
23
]
and also inconsistent definition
[
38
]
.

Rahman, et al.
[
107
]
showed that intra- and inter-observer reproducibility had an average difference of 23
\mu
m and 32
\mu
m, respectively, while Shao, et al.
[
108
]
showed good agreement between graders of around 3
\mu
m on average. Boonarpha, et al.
[
23
]
showed that the repeatability of manual choroid thickness measurements vary according to image quality, ranging from a mean difference between raters of 4
\mu
m and 27
\mu
m across the spectrum of Choroid-Sclera boundary visualisation. Sim, et al.
[
109
]
found that inter-rater variability in thickness from two graders improved from 6 – 40
\mu
m on average to 4 – 21
\mu
m after introducing a strict manual measurement protocol for individual Sattler, Haller and choroid layer segmentation.

Yiu, et al.
[
19
]
found that varying the definition of the Choroid-Sclera boundary to include/exclude the suprachoroidal space lead to error in manual
SFCT
ranging from 17
\mu
m to 70
\mu
m. Chandrasekera, et al.
[
38
]
showed that agreement between automatic and manual detection of the Choroid-Sclera boundary worsened when the suprachoroidal space was visible (increase of mean difference in thickness of 17
\mu
m to 23
\mu
m). Conversely, Vuong, et al.
[
110
]
found that only 15% of 91 eyes had a visible suprachoroidal space in a population with average age 59.5, and that it’s appearance did not impact manual measurement error, which ranged between 1 – 37
\mu
m on average when considering different Choroid-Sclera boundary definitions.

Given that effect size can vary across disease, with some being as low as 20 – 30
\mu
m change (such as when tracking myopia progression
[
25
,
111
]
), the wide variability in reproducibility from manual grading from poor measurement protocol and human bias
[
23
,
24
]
poses a problem for standardised choroidal image analysis in
OCT
image sequences.

Additionally, measuring choroidal thickness on
OCT
has also been poorly standardised in the research community, as the choroid can appear skewed on an
OCT
B-scan and some institutions account for this skew
[
20
,
23
,
60
,
112
]
, while others do not
[
14
,
19
,
22
,
65
,
107
,
113
]
. We will show in section
Choroidal Image Analysis for OCT Image Sequences with Applications in Systemic Health
that accounting for this choroidal curvature can have an inordinate impact on measuring choroidal thickness because of the pixel-to-micron scaling imposed by the
OCT
B-scan. We will also show in chapter
Choroidal Image Analysis for OCT Image Sequences with Applications in Systemic Health
that manual graders are a poor reference standard for measuring choroid thickness consistently within patients when accounting for this choroidal curvature.

{mysubsubsection}

[]Semi- and Fully-automatic

The inconsistencies introduced from manual grading has been a driving force for more objective approaches based on computer science and mathematics, with traditional image processing methods initially playing a significant role. These methods often required manual intervention or pre-defined parameters, making them semi-automatic and less efficient compared to more modern, fully automatic methods which use deep learning. Table
gives a brief overview of the range of semi-automatic and fully-automatic approaches developed for choroid region segmentation over the last 12 years. Performance metrics have explicitly not been included due to lack of publicly available datasets to standardise and benchmark performance with.

Briefly, many semi-automatic approaches utilised graph-based methods such as Dijkstra’s algorithm
[
114
]
for individual edge tracing of the RPE-Choroid and Choroid-Sclera boundaries. These methods represent the image as a graph, where each pixel is a node, and the segmentation task is modelled as a graph partitioning problem
[
13
,
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
,
119
,
120
,
121
,
122
,
123
]
. Other traditional, semi-automatic approaches leveraged statistical models based on level sets and different enhancement strategies to simplify the edge tracing problem
[
124
,
125
,
126
,
127
,
128
,
129
]
. While these semi-automatic approaches improve on just manual labelling alone, they typically rely on handcrafted features and pre-defined criteria which lack generalisation. Additionally, many of these approaches take a local perspective on optimisation or require many tuning parameters and thus domain-specific knowledge to operate successfully. Moreover, these methods are multi-stage and heavily rely on many pre-processing steps which, if sub-optimal, can affect the output segmentation.

As datasets grew and computational resources became more available, fully automatic approaches based on convolutional neural networks (
CNN
s) become preferable over semi-automatic ones and could provide faster and more generalisable inference
[
130
]
. The earlier approaches were a combination of feature extracting
CNN
s which took a patch-based binary classification approach to region segmentation, utilising semi-automatic algorithms for pre-/post-processing and model fitting
[
131
,
132
,
133
,
134
,
135
,
136
,
137
,
138
]
. More recently, there has been a push to develop fully automatic methods using fully convolutional networks (FCNs), such as the popularised UNet deep learning architecture
[
139
]
with residual
[
140
]
or dense learning
[
141
]
. This permits pixel-level segmentation and Kugelman, et al.
[
142
]
found that this type of encoder–decoder deep learning architecture outperformed patch-based methods. Thus, the UNet model has become the reference standard for deep learning approaches toward choroid region segmentation
[
16
,
143
,
144
,
145
,
146
,
147
,
148
]
.

{mysubsection}

[]Vessel segmentation

While manual segmentation of the choroidal region requires identification of a single landmark in the OCT B-scan, segmenting the choroidal vessels require multiple, interdependent and heterogeneous blobs to be identified which is prohibitively time consuming and subject to human bias. Thus, approaches toward choroid vessel segmentation leveraged semi-automatic and fully-automatic approaches from the get-go. Table
gives a brief overview of the range of semi-automatic and fully-automatic approaches developed for choroid vessel segmentation over the last 12 years. Performance metrics have explicitly not been included due to lack of publicly available datasets to standardise and benchmark performance with.

Some of the earliest methods were oriented around binarisation and thresholding, whereby choroidal pixels are classified based on pixel intensity values into choroid vessel and surrounding interstitial tissue (choroid stroma)
[
105
,
153
]
. Other methods were more domain-specific to address the heterogeneity of the choroidal vessel size and contrast
[
154
,
155
,
156
,
157
]
but were heavily inter-dependent on a priori information such vessel shape.

Local thresholding methods were preferred over global ones as an attempt to address the heterogeneity in size of the choroidal vasculature. Namely, the Niblack autolocal thresholding algorithm
[
162
]
became commonly used in the research community by virtue of two research groups
[
28
,
159
]
releasing measurement protocols for Fiji (ImageJ)
[
30
]
or membership-only image analysis platforms
[
161
]
which use modified versions of Niblack. These semi-automatic approaches suffer from manual intervention as part of the protocol, thus making large-scale volumetric analysis unfeasible. Therefore, some attempts have been made to replace semi-automatic algorithms using deep learning
[
160
]
, with UNet architectures having superior performance over SegNet
[
150
]
and DeepLab
[
163
]
models, according to ground-truth labels generated by Niblack’s method, as reported by Muller, et al.
[
164
]
.

{mysubsection}

[]Synthesising region and vessel segmentation

All of the previous approaches have considered the tasks of region and vessel segmentations as separate ones. However, it is advantageous to consider the inter-dependence of the choroidal space and vessels during the modelling process, and recent advancements have focused on synergistically segmenting both the choroidal region and its vasculature to provide more comprehensive analyses of the choroid. Table
gives a brief overview of the range of semi-automatic and fully-automatic approaches developed for choroid region and vessel segmentation. Performance metrics have explicitly not been included due to lack of publicly available datasets to standardise and benchmark performance with.

Traditional methods have been proposed for choroid layer and vessel segmentation
[
120
,
165
]
, but were complex, multi-stage and heavily parameter dependent. Attempts have been made at deep learning-based segmentation of Haller’s and Sattler’s layers
[
166
]
, despite the oblique presentation of choroidal vessels on OCT and no reliable ground truth or standardised protocol to differentiate the two vascular layers
[
9
]
. Other deep learning methods segmented the region and vessels but still considered their problem formulations as separate entities
[
18
,
167
,
168
]
, applying semi-automatic Niblack’s method for vessel segmentation. Although, Xuan, et al.
[
18
]
did acknowledge the need for standardisation of choroidal measurements and included additional
OCT
B-scan foveal pit detection in their model DCAP.

Finally, more comprehensive approaches combined the tasks of region and vessel segmentation into single deep learning-based models, with the creation of models based on UNet architectures
[
139
]
like ChoroidNet
[
169
]
CUNet
[
170
]
and CVI-Net
[
171
]
. A final advancement was the development of transformer-assisted TACLNet, proposed by Wen, et al.
[
173
]
which showed superiority performance across CUNet
[
170
]
, ChoroidNet
[
169
]
, CVI-Net
[
171
]
and the regular UNet
[
139
]
.

{mysubsection}

[]Current challenges

{mysubsubsection}

[]Vessel segmentation

Regarding choroid vessel segmentation, the research community largely adopt the semi-automatic vessel segmentation protocols outlined by Sonoda, et al.
[
28
]
and Agrawal, et al.
[
159
]
which both use the semi-automatic Niblack thresholding algorithm. However, despite the widespread use of Niblack’s algorithm for choroid vessel segmentation, there is concern surrounding its improper usage. A crucial element missing from the established software and measurement protocols
[
28
,
161
]
is how to use the Niblack method and, specifically, how to tune the internal parameters of the local thresholding tool. The platforms available to conduct image analysis, such as Fiji (ImageJ)
[
30
]
, have further compounded this issue, as they often default to specific parameter settings that may not be reported by researchers. To the best of our knowledge, only two studies have reported specific Niblack parameters
[
164
,
168
]
. This lack of transparency makes it difficult to compare results across different studies or to replicate findings, thus hampering efforts to standardise the reporting of choroidal measurements.

There have also been several other reports hindering the standardisation of Niblack’s autolocal thresholding algorithm
[
164
,
174
,
175
,
176
,
177
]
. Wei, et al.
[
174
]
found a reported difference of around 3% in choroid vessel density (and an intra-class correlation of 0.35) measured following the protocols outlined by Sonoda, et al.
[
28
]
and Agrawal, et al.
[
159
]
. Elbay, et al.
[
175
]
observed the modified Niblack method proposed by Agrawal, et al.
[
159
]
to be sensitive to the type of scaling used to view the
OCT
B-scan (see section
Choroidal Image Analysis for OCT Image Sequences with Applications in Systemic Health
), reporting errors of up to 4% in choroid vessel density. Muller, et al.
[
164
]
found that tuning of only one of the internal Niblack parameters could result in differences in choroid vessel density between 2% and 20%. Rosa, et al.
[
176
]
found a significant difference of around 7.5% in choroid vessel density when using the Niblack method proposed by Agrawal, et al.
[
159
]
and altering the image brightness of the
OCT
B-scan. Finally, Ma, et al.
[
177
]
reported that the mean difference between
SD-OCT
and
SS-OCT
data in three dimensional vessel density was 13% using the protocol outlined by Sonoda, et al.
[
28
]
.

These are crucial studies which accompany the concern around the Niblack method’s potential lack of reproducibility and measurement error. Agrawal, et al.
[
29
]
published a major review on choroid vessel density in retinal and systemic disease reporting average changes between healthy and diseased eyes between 2% and 6%. This suggests that improper usage of these current methods and their lack of standardisation could result in measurement error exceeding observed effect sizes in disease, which could lead to paradoxical results inherently skewed by measurement variability. This was posited by Rosa, et al.
[
176
]
who, in their discussion, found contradictory results among the literature when measuring the choroid vessel density in Vogt-Koyanagi-Harada disease
[
178
,
179
,
180
,
181
]
and Non-Arteritic Anterior Ischemic Optic Neuropathy
[
182
,
183
]
, using the methods outlined by Sonoda, et al.
[
28
]
and Agrawal, et al.
[
159
]
.

Conversely to Ma, et al.
[
177
]
, Agrawal, et al.
[
184
]
reported a mean difference in choroid vessel density of only 3% between two-dimensional
SD-OCT
and
SS-OCT
data. While not perfectly comparable to three-dimensional vessel density, recent evidence suggest that choroid vessel density does not change across different macular scanning locations
[
185
,
186
,
187
]
(but does between macular and peripheral locations
[
188
]
). Thus, it is possible that the contradictory findings between Ma, et al.
[
177
]
and Agrawal, et al.
[
184
]
may be linked to improper use of Niblack’s method, such that it may be sufficient to measure the choroidal vessels only with suitable domain knowledge and expertise of the algorithm. Given that Agrawal, et al.
[
184
]
developed and released the modified Niblack method, it’s likely they know how best to use it. Unfortunately, there lacks a standardised protocol for using and specifying internal parameters for semi-automatic methods like Niblack which is so heavily adopted in the community
[
29
]
. It would be pertinent for one to exist to educate researchers on the pitfalls of its parameter specification, similar to Chu, et al.
[
189
]
who do so for using the Phansalkar thresholding algorithm for the choriocapillaris in
OCT-A
.

{mysubsubsection}

[]Bias, reproducibility and accessibility

A problem in the literature is the generation of high quality ground truth data for deep learning model development, particularly for choroid vessel segmentation. The majority of the sophisticated deep learning-based are developed and evaluated against manual ground truth labels which are subject to human bias (tables
–
, Data column). Few consider the value of developing models based on ground truth labels generated using semi-automatic or fully automatic approaches, which explicitly encode a degree of determinism and consistency into the ground truth generation process. Maloca, et al.
[
190
]
argues that such approaches to generating ground truth labels are preferable as they reduce subjectivity and thus bias and inconsistency, especially for the choroid.

Furthermore, there is a distinct lack of reporting the reproducibility of automatic methods in medical imaging research and deep learning-based segmentation (tables
–
, Reproducibility column, only 9 (18%) out of all 49 reviewed papers conducted reproducibility analysis). For those studies which do report reproducibility, they typically adopt population-level, dimensionless metrics like the intra-class correlation or coefficient of variation/repeatability. Even unit-dependent metrics like average signed differences can be subject to cancellation effects and asymmetric error distributions. Without an
interpretable
and
contextual
assessment of reproducibility, there is no way to differentiate measurement variability from the true biological effect being measured. This is particularly pertinent for interpreting study findings where effect sizes are likely to be small, such as in choroid vessel density between healthy and disease eyes (2% – 6%)
[
29
]
or choroid thickness in myopia progression (20 – 30
\mu
m)
[
25
,
111
]
.

There are currently no accessible, open-source algorithms for large-scale, fully-automatic choroid region and vessel segmentation (tables
–
, Open-source column, only 5 (10%) out of all 49 reviewed papers released their codebase). All available algorithms fall into one of three categories: First, manual or semi-automatic methods
[
27
,
28
,
30
,
159
,
191
]
that require human supervision (introducing subjectivity) and/or membership-access only. Second, fully-automatic deep learning-based methods that are not openly accessible, either only providing the code but not the trained model necessary to use the method
[
142
,
148
]
or not providing any access at the time of writing
[
18
]
(December 2024). Third, releasing the model as fully-automatic but comprising of many steps, requiring underlying domain-specific knowledge or a license for proprietary software
[
13
,
168
]
.

{mysubsection}

[]Summary

The field of choroid region and vessel segmentation has seen significant evolution. Beginning from traditional image processing like graph-based
[
13
,
118
,
120
]
and thresholding methods
[
28
,
29
,
159
]
, which suffer from domain-specific expertise, free-parameter tuning, inter-dependent pipelines and limitations on generalisability to complex edge-cases, and ending at advanced, fully-automatic deep learning-based methods, which offered superior accuracy and large-scale automation
[
130
]
, marking a significant advancement in the field of ophthalmic image analysis. While initially these methods treated the two tasks separately
[
16
,
147
,
148
,
160
]
, researchers leveraged deep learning methods for synergistic segmentation of the region and vessels
[
18
,
169
,
170
,
171
,
173
]
.

However, these methods are still limited. Their ground truth label generation is still subject to human subjectivity when produced manually, and there is a lack of interpretable reproducibility analysis to help the research community differentiate measurement variability from true biological change. Additionally, there is a significant absence of open-source, deep learning methods available to the wider research community. This has resulted in much of the research community still dependent on manual or semi-automatic methods which are made available by image analysis platforms like Fiji (ImageJ)
[
30
]
. This can have unfortunate consequences regarding the standardisation of choroidal measurements in
OCT
image sequences, particularly if different definitions of the Choroid-Sclera boundary are assumed
[
19
,
23
,
24
,
38
]
, or improper usage of Niblack’s method continues
[
164
,
174
,
175
,
176
,
177
]
.

{mysection}

[]Measuring the choroidal microvasculature

Figure 10
:
EDI-OCT
B-scan showing the choroid beneath the retina, posterior to the hyperreflective outer retinal layers and fixed to the
RPE
. Red-dashed lines mark the
RPE
-Choroid and Choroid-Sclera boundaries, with annotated landmarks. Due to the limited resolution of current
OCT
devices (about 3.87
\mu
m-per-pixel for Heidelberg Engineering Spectralis
OCT
1 devices), the choriocapillaris layer is barely visible. The choroidal space appears as bright regions (stroma) with darker areas representing vasculature, a pattern empirically observed
[
104
,
105
]
and accepted in the research community
[
29
]
.

Figure 11
:
OCT B-scan with visible suprachoroidal space, with coloured arrows corresponding to the three distinct definitions of the Choroid-Sclera boundary.

Figure
10
shows an exemplar
EDI-OCT
B-scan with the RPE-Choroid and Choroid-Sclera boundaries delineating the choroidal space, with important biological landmarks commonly captured in the B-scan also visually defined. Conceptually, there there is debate around the exact location of the Choroid-Sclera boundary, and figure
11
demonstrates this. Depending on the appearance of the choroid (and the visibility of the suprachoroidal layer) in an
OCT
B-scan, this may either be
[
19
]
:

1.

the hyperreflective line at the posterior most location of the largest choroidal vessels in Haller’s layer, i.e. inner choroid junction (figure
11
, blue arrow);

2.

the hyporeflective line separating the posterior of Haller’s layer from the suprachoroid, i.e. inner suprachoroid junction (figure
11
, yellow arrow);

3.

the hyperreflective line separating the suprachoroidal layer from the sclera, i.e. inner scleral junction (figure
11
, green arrow).

The disparity in these definitions can have an inordinate impact on choroidal measurements — Yiu, et al.
[
19
]
found that error in manual
SFCT
can vary from 17
\mu
m to 70
\mu
m when different definitions of the Choroid-Sclera boundary were used, especially when the suprachoroidal layer was observed.

The original authors who proposed
EDI-OCT

[
12
]
define the choroid to include the suprachoroidal layer
[
9
]
, and thus define the Choroid-Sclera boundary at the point of the inner sclera
[
192
]
, i.e. definition (3) (green arrow in figure
11
). However, even that definition is still debated and can be hard to discern in practice, as the suprachoroidal space is not always present due to
OCT
device limitations, age, health and other factors
[
19
]
. Inconsistency arises when certain choroids exhibit a suprachoroidal space and others do not, under the assumption that this space has no relationship to the research objective
[
19
,
38
]
.

Chandrasekera, et al.
[
38
]
showed that only 38% of 200 eyes showed a visible suprachoroid in
SS-OCT
data, and Yiu, et al.
[
19
]
showed a similar proportion (45%) in 74 eyes using
EDI-OCT
data, noting that visibility of the suprachoroid was higher in older eyes. Additionally, both studies found that choroidal thickness was significantly different when considering the different definitions of the choroid outlined above. Moreover, Chandrasekera, et al.
[
38
]
additionally found that agreement between semi-automatic and manual detection of the Choroid-Sclera boundary worsened when the suprachoroidal space was visible (increased mean difference in thickness of 17
\mu
m to 23
\mu
m), reporting that automatic methods would prefer definition (1) while manual measurement would opt for definition (3).

Thus, to remain objective in our analyses throughout this thesis — given the inconsistent visibility of the suprachoroidal layer, and the fact that it is an avascular potential space
[
193
]
unlike the highly vascularised choroidal layers
[
8
]
— we opted to follow definition (1) (blue arrow in figure
11
).

Therefore, the choroidal space in an
OCT
B-scan for this thesis is defined as the space posterior to the boundary delineating the
RPE
layer and Bruch’s membrane complex (
RPE
-Choroid) and superior to the boundary delineating the sclera or suprachoroid, if visible, from the posterior most point of Haller’s layer (Choroid-Sclera).

{mysubsection}

[]OCT B-scan

Figure 12
:
(A)
OCT
B-scan with measurement landmarks overlaid to describe construction of the fovea-centred
ROI
. (B)
OCT
B-scan with prescribed
ROI
in red,
SFCT
in black, and vessels in purple.

Measuring the choroid in the macula is usually done with reference to a particular region of interest (
ROI
). This is to ensure comparisons between and within patients can be done accurately and reliably. For the work in this thesis, we consider measuring the macular choroid in a fovea-centred
ROI
, with some micron distance either side of the fovea. The specific location of the fovea is its centre, at the foveal pit. The foveal pit in an
OCT
B-scan is defined in this thesis as a single pixel coordinate at the centre of the foveola centralis. The foveola is a circular zone of approximately 350 micron width which is avascular, creating a depression at the centre of the macula which pushes the inner retinal layers laterally
[
194
]
. The single pixel coordinate is one which appears most brightly illuminated on the B-scan in this depression, often aligning with a ridge formed at the photoreceptor layer.

Figure
12
demonstrates the construction of a two-dimensional
ROI
in an
OCT
B-scan (A), with measurements visualised in (B). In panel (A), we show the landmarks used to define a two-dimensional
ROI
(red), given a 3000
\mu
m distance either side of the fovea and describe its construction below:

1.

Given the location of the fovea (red cross in
12
(A)), find the nearest location along the
RPE
-Choroid boundary, which defines the centre of the
ROI
(cyan cross in
12
(A)).

2.

Traversing the
RPE
-Choroid boundary some micron distance (3000
\mu
m in this example) either side of this central pixel defines the endpoints of the
ROI
(purple crosses in
12
(A)) along this boundary.

3.

Tangential lines at each
RPE
-Choroid reference point are drawn (black dashed lines in
12
(A)), using an offset of 15 pixels in each direction to the reference point to estimate tangential gradient around reference points.

4.

Tangential lines are rotated 90 degrees (black solid lines in
12
(A)) and extended until they intersect the Choroid-Sclera boundary, detecting the reference points along this boundary (blue crosses in
12
(A)).

The
ROI
is defined as the choroidal space (red shaded region in
12
(A)) which is bound between these solid black lines which extend
locally perpendicular
from the
RPE
-Choroid boundary. Importantly, the prescribed, fovea-centred
ROI
accounts for any choroidal curvature from poor acquisition, or degree of myopia, and ensures a standardised measurement protocol for measuring the choroid.

In figure
12
(B), we show the 3000
\mu
m, fovea-centred
ROI
in red, with segmented vessels in this
ROI
in blue, and a black solid line representing the one-dimensional, subfoveal choroid thickness (
SFCT
) for this B-scan. Within this
ROI
, there are four metrics of interest to measure the choroid:
SFCT
, choroid area, choroid vascular area and choroid vascular index (
CVI
).
SFCT
measures the thickness of the choroid directly underneath the fovea, and locally perpendicular to the
RPE
-Choroid boundary (black solid line in figure
12
(B)). Using the pixel length-scales in the axial and lateral directions, the pixel length of this black line is converted into microns. It is most common to measure the choroid through a one-dimensional measurement like
SFCT

[
11
]
. However, because of the segmental nature of the choroidal vascular bed
[
45
]
, this is misguided
[
84
]
. Thus, it is common to take several thickness measurements across the
ROI
and average them
[
24
]
.

Instead of one-dimensional thickness, two-dimensional measurements like area are more representative and robust measurements of the choroid
[
24
]
. Choroid area counts the number of pixels in the total
ROI
(red and purple pixels in figure
12
(B)), and this count is converted into mm
2
by a conversion factor equal to the total mm
2
area of a single pixel, dictated by the axial and lateral pixel length-scales. Choroid vessel area is computed much the same but only for the vessel pixels (purple pixels in
12
(B)).
CVI
is a dimensionless ratio of choroid vascular area to total choroid area within the prescribed
ROI
(ratio of purple pixels to red and purple pixels in figure
12
(B)).

{mysubsubsection}

[]Pixel length-scales

Figure 13
:
Relationship between the scan focus value and the lateral (A) and frontal (B) pixel length-scales for Heidelberg Engineering
OCT
systems.

The axial and lateral pixel length-scales in an
OCT
B-scan are typically not equal, which is known as an
anisotropic
scaling. For
OCT
imaging devices, the ratio of axial to lateral pixel length-scale is approximately 1:3. Heidelberg Engineering’s Spectralis
OCT
1 module has a fixed axial pixel length-scale of 3.87
\mu
m-per-pixel and, for a perfectly emmetropic model eye, a lateral pixel length-scale of 11.47
\mu
m-per-pixel for pixel resolution 768
\times
768. Topcon’s DRI Triton Plus has a fixed axial pixel length-scale of 2.61
\mu
m-per-pixel and a fixed lateral pixel length-scale of 8.79
\mu
m-per-pixel for pixel resolution 992
\times
1024.

For some
OCT
imaging devices (Heidelberg Engineering’s
OCT
Spectralis, for example), the lateral pixel length-scale has the ability to change across different examinations depending on the corneal surface properties and refraction of the patient’s eye, as well as the operators use of the machine. While the former can be approximately encoded into the device, this is not always known and thus a phantom eye model is used instead. In the latter, during acquisition the operator can adjust various settings to compensate for the individual refractive surfaces in each patient’s eye
[
103
]
.

An important tuning parameter during acquisition for the technician is the scan focus knob on the imaging device. This compensates for the refractive error of the patient’s eye, and thus acts as an approximation for the patient’s refractive error (measured in dioptres, D). While suitable focus on the chorioretinal structures is possible with the scan focus knob set to ‘0’ — perfectly emmetropic — it can be adjusted freely and is particularly useful in cases of high myopia or hyperopia
[
103
]
. Adjusting the focus alters the lateral pixel length-scale in an
OCT
B-scan, as well as the distance between sequential B-scans in an
OCT
volume scan (the frontal plane length-scale). This relationship can be observed in figure
13
, which is approximately linear in the emmetropic range, between -1D and 1D. This is a crucial detail for producing standardised choroidal measurements, and can in particular have significant consequences for measurements which are computed using different
ROI
definitions.

{mysubsubsection}

[]Different viewing modes

Figure 14
:
An
EDI-OCT
B-scan presented in 1:1 pixel mode (A) and 1:1 micron mode (B).

A way to circumvent anisotropic scaling is to present the chorioretinal structures in a B-scan whose axial and lateral pixel length-scales are equal. Default scaling used by most in the research community presents the
OCT
B-scan in ‘1:1 pixel mode’ (figure
14
(A)), allowing chorioretinal structures to be visualised more easily for the end-user by appearing more bloated in the axial direction which aids the interpretation and readability of the B-scan. Another option available to present the chorioretinal structures is ‘1:1 micron mode’ (figure
14
(B)) which, although squashes the chorioretinal structures axially, is advised by device manufacturers to help mitigate for potential manual measurement error
[
195
]
.

Indeed, Lawali, et al.
[
196
]
found that different scale types produced significantly different subfoveal retinal thickness measurements when measured manually. However, with the introduction of automatic methods which can segment and subsequently measure the chorioretinal anatomy in a programmatic way, presenting the B-scan using the default ‘1:1 pixel mode’ is sufficient, and is used for the remainder of this thesis. However, it is for images with anisotropic scaling that the anatomic curvature is compensated for at the point of measurement.

{mysubsubsection}

SFCT
. Green pixels represent overlap between the two image- and choroid-aligned
ROI
s, with red and blue pixels representing regions exclusive to the image- and choroid-aligned
ROI
