Table 1
:
Choroidal measurements for the flat, skewed, curved and skewed-and-curved choroids from figure
15
, while simulating different refractive errors. Highly myopic: -9.17D, emmetropic: 0D, highly hyperopic: 7.37D.
\Delta_{r}
is absolute, relative difference measured as
\nicefrac{{|c-i|}}{{i}}
where
c
and
i
are the choroid- and image-aligned values.
\Delta
is absolute difference.

Historically, retinochoroidal metrics are measured according to the image-axis
[
14
,
19
,
22
,
65
,
107
,
113
]
. That is, the
ROI
and subsequent measurements are defined and computed per A-scan (column-wise) which most imaging devices also adopt. However, this assumes there is little to no curvature in the retinochoroidal tissue in the B-scan. The shape of the patient’s eye (degree of myopia) and quality of image capture both contribute to the appearance of the retina and choroid on the
OCT
B-scan. This is why in this thesis we ensure the
ROI
, and subsequent measurements, are choroid-aligned, i.e. locally perpendicular to the
RPE
-Choroid boundary. This
ROI
is aligned with the predominant axis which the choroid’s
RPE
-Choroid boundary lies along, which can deviate from the lateral image-axis. This deviation is known as
skew
or
curvature
, defined as consistent or inconsistent deviation in the angle of elevation of the choroid from the lateral image-axis, respectively.

Figure
15
shows
OCT
B-scans whose intra-ocular structures exhibit different types of skew or curvature. The choroid in panel (A) appears predominantly flat, while the skewed choroid in panel (B) shows consistent deviation away from the horizontal image-axis. The choroid in panel (C) is curved and thus shows inconsistent deviation from the horizontal image-axis. In panel (D) we see both skew and curvature.

In figure
15
, we overlay the image-aligned and choroid-aligned
ROI
s and
SFCT
. While the majority of the
ROI
s overlap (green), there are distinct regions which are mutually exclusive between the
ROI
definitions which, depending on the extent of skew and/or curvature, can have an impact on downstream choroidal measurements. For example, the skew exhibited by the choroids in panels (B, D) underneath and fovea result in deviation between the lines drawn measuring
SFCT
when considering different
ROI
s (red solid line compared to blue dashed-line). This is in contrast to the choroids in panels (A, C), which are locally flat underneath the fovea, resulting in identical lines drawn to measure
SFCT
regardless of the
ROI
definition.

The extent of deviation in choroidal measurements was investigated for these four examples by considering a fovea-centred
ROI
covering a 6000
\mu
m distance and measuring
SFCT
, average thickness across the
ROI
, total choroid area and
CVI
. For these four eyes, we consider three cases where the patient’s eye is highly myopic, emmetropic and highly hyperopic, simulating different lateral pixel length-scales (for a Heidelberg Engineering
OCT
imaging device). We define emmetropic as when the scan focus is 0, which corresponds to a lateral scaling of 11.47
\mu
m-per-pixel. We define highly myopic and hyperopic as the extremal observations from figure
13
, i.e. refractive errors (approximated by the scan focus knob in dioptres, D) -9.17D and 7.37D, respectively. These correspond to lateral pixel length-scales of 13.51
\mu
m-per-pixel and 10.24
\mu
m-per-pixel, respectively.

Table
1
shows the extent of deviation between the image- and choroid-aligned choroidal measurements. As expected, measurements were stable for the flat choroid, but significant deviations in
SFCT
and average thickness was observed across the board for the remaining choroids (the curved choroid did not deviate in
SFCT
because it is locally flat under the fovea as in figure
15
(C)). Interestingly, deviation in all choroidal measurements (but primarily in
SFCT
and average thickness) increased as the eye became more myopic. This is due to the anisotropic scaling which becomes more disproportionate with increasing degree of myopia (figure
13
). This suggests not only the importance of ensuring a consistent
ROI
definition, but also that these pixel length-scales are taken into account when reporting physical measurements of populations which could have a wide variety of refractive errors.

For the skewed choroids, which were not aligned with the image axis underneath the fovea, the average
SFCT
deviation was 16% (37
\mu
m in absolute value), and 31% (58
\mu
m in absolute value) for average thickness across the
ROI
. Across all choroids, there was a deviation in average thickness of 19% (37
\mu
m in absolute value) and the largest deviation observed was the skewed choroid for a hypothetically myopic eye, which saw a deviation of 23% and 54% (79
\mu
m and 86
\mu
m in absolute value) for
SFCT
and average thickness, respectively.

Two-dimensional measurements like area and
CVI
appear more robust to these different
ROI
definitions, with an average absolute difference in
CVI
of 0.006 (0.6%) across all choroids. For area, choroidal curvature did cause some minor deviation of 2% (0.026 mm
2
in absolute value) for the curved choroids.

While the deviations in two dimensional measurements are unlikely to be clinically significant (Agrawal, et al.
[
29
]
reported differences in
CVI
of between 2% – 6% between healthy and diseased eyes), the reported deviations in thickness are. For example, reported effect sizes in choroidal thickness can be as low as 20 – 30
\mu
m when considering myopia progression
[
111
,
25
]
, while changes in
SFCT
is reported to decrease between 5% – 12% during haemodialysis, as reported by Farrah, et al.
[
62
]
, and choroidal thinning by approximately 25% in end-stage
CKD
, as reported by Balmforth, et al.
[
60
]
.

Figure 16
:
A selection of
OCT
B-scans which show varying curvature and skew, with their refractive error annotated.

SFCT
is a one-dimensional straight line distance measure which is highly susceptible to changes in anisotropic pixel length-scales. As will be discussed in chapter
Choroidal Image Analysis for OCT Image Sequences with Applications in Systemic Health
, this anisotropic scaling and difficulty to follow standardised measurement protocol can have an inordinate impact on comparing measurements between and within patients. Two-dimensional measurements showed less bias because of a potential cancellation effect between the different
ROI
s. For example, in figure
15
(B), the red and blue shaded regions form two distinct triangles either side of the overlapped portion of each
ROI
(shaded green region), and thus contribute similar amounts to their respective image- and choroid-aligned area value, respectively. However, in cases where there is choroidal curvature, this cancellation effect is lost and can cause measurement error like the choroid in figure
15
(C) where area deviated most (table
1
).

Figure
16
shows six
OCT
B-scans from eyes with varying refractive error — measured using the Oculus Myopia Master (Oculus, Wetzlar, Germany) — visualising the different kinds of curvature and skew that can occur across the spectrum of refractive error. Skewedness of the intra-ocular structures is primarily due to poor acquisition, and curvature from high myopia
[
197
]
. However, modern day
OCT
image capture has added functionality to circumvent choroidal curvature and high myopia
[
195
]
. This can be seen in panel (B), whose eye had a refractive error of approximately 8D, but showed no signs of curvature. This is in contrast to the degree of curvature and skew observed in panel (A) for the eye whose refractive error was -10D. The intra-ocular structures are skewed in panels (C) and (D) from poor acquisition, while in panel (E) we see significant curvature for an emmetropic eye (-0.29D). Finally, in panel (F) we see that for this hyperopic eye (6.15D), the choroid is large and the intra-ocular structures are flattened due to the size of the eye. These examples suggest that the extent of curvature and skewedness can occur across the range of refractive errors.

{mysubsection}

[]OCT volume scan

The introduction of automatic segmentation methods has permitted near real-time segmentation of the choroid across macular
OCT
volume scans. Measuring the macular choroid is a more representative quantification than subfoveal choroid thickness
[
24
]
, allowing choroid volume and thickness to be estimated at different locations in the macula.

An
OCT
volume is made up of several, parallel and linear
OCT
B-scans spaced evenly across the macula. Given an
OCT
volume whose choroid has been fully segmented, measurements across the macular choroid can be estimated by interpolating choroid-derived thickness and volume (and vessel density) across the B-scans. This constitutes generating a two-dimensional thickness map which encodes the depth of the choroid at each location on the macula and en face localiser.

Figure 17
:
Procedure to generate a choroid thickness map. (A)
OCT
volume scan with segmentations overlaid. (B) Valid thicknesses measured for the choroid across the volume scan. (C) Thickness array alignment using fovea. (D) Step-wise process to generate thickness map. (E) Final thickness (heat)map overlaid onto en face localiser, with redder colours implying higher depth/density.

The procedure generating the thickness map is shown in figure
17
(D) and described below. the procedure for measuring choroid vessel maps is analogous.

1.

For every B-scan, thickness measurements are taken at every available A-scan, measuring the micron distance between the
RPE
-Choroid and Choroid-Sclera boundary, locally perpendicular to the
RPE
-Choroid boundary (figure
17
(B));

2.

Each B-scans’ thickness array is aligned (figure
17
(C)) with the lateral position of the fovea detected on the en face localiser (red dotted and solid lines in figures
17
(B) and
17
(C), respectively). Thickness array alignment per B-scan is required since the anatomical layer segmentations (black diagonal lines in figure
17
(C)) do not necessarily cover the full lateral width of the acquisition region of interest (green diagonal lines in figure
17
(C));

3.

The aligned thickness arrays are stacked along the frontal plane to create a coarse, two-dimensional map of thickness values (
17
(Di));

4.

This is padded horizontally by duplicating the edge values to prevent edge artefacts from interpolation (
17
(Dii));

5.

This is then interpolated to the same pixel resolution as the SLO localiser using bi-linear interpolation (
17
(Diii));

6.

Since the raw thickness arrays only sample the depth of the choroid at discrete locations, a Gaussian filter whose standard deviation equals the frontal-plane pixel resolution — the pixel distance between sequential B-scans — is applied for smoothing (
17
(Div));

7.

The smoothed map is vertically padded to centre the map onto the fovea of the en face localiser SLO, and the padded edges from step (2) are removed (
17
(Dv));

8.

Finally, this map is rotated to the angle of elevation of the acquisition
ROI
(
17
(Dvi)), as shown by the green and black diagonal lines in figure
17
(C).

Figure 18
:
Diagram of how measurements are computed for a macular
OCT
volume scan. (A) 3D render of an
OCT
volume scan. (B) Sequential B-scans with segmentations overlaid. (C) Choroid thickness, vessel density and
CVI
(heat)maps, with average measurements along the
ETDRS
grid overlaid (redder colours imply higher depth/density).

The thickness map (in microns) can then be interpolated to estimate choroidal volume (in mm
3
), or can be averaged for more localised measurements. Choroid vessels maps of interest include vessel density in micron
2
, volume in mm
3
and
CVI
(dimensionless).

A standard macular grid was developed over 30 years ago from a landmark, multi-centre clinical trial the Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (
ETDRS
)
[
198
]
. The
ETDRS
grid centres three concentric circles with 1mm, 3mm and 6mm diameters at the foveal pit as seen on the en face localiser, and further splits the 3mm and 6mm circles into four, equally sized quadrants. The quadrants are aligned with the angle of elevation of the
OCT
volume’s acquisition
ROI
. Average thickness, vessel density,
CVI
and interpolated volumes are measured in these 9 sub-fields. Figure
18
shows representative choroid thickness, vessel density and
CVI
maps across the macula, generated from an OCT volume scan, superimposed onto the en face localiser with measurements in each sub-field of the
ETDRS
grid.

{mysubsection}

[]OCT peripapillary scan

Figure 19
:
Diagram demonstrating how peripapillary thickness measurements for an
OCT
peripapillary B-scan are made. (A) Circular
OCT
B-scan overlaid onto the en face localiser. (B) Detection of the centre of the temporal peripapillary grid. (C) Peripapillary grid definition on the en face localiser, with average choroid thickness values overlaid per sub-field. (D) Circular
OCT
B-scan with choroid layer segmentation overlaid.

A peripapillary B-scan is a circular scan centred at the optic disc and is continuous along the frontal plane (the lateral ends of the B-scan are continuous). Figure
19
shows the procedure to obtain average choroid thickness in each of the peripapillary sub-fields, often used for assessing glaucoma
[
199
]
. These sub-fields measure chorioretinal thickness spatially around the optic disc, as the circular B-scan traverses the disc’s circumference at an offset. Panel (A) shows the en face localiser with the circular B-scan overlaid.

Upon choroid layer segmentation of the choroid in a peripapillary
OCT
B-scan (figure
19
(D)), we first generate a thickness array. This measures a micron distance for every A-scan (column). We do not measure locally perpendicular to the RPE-Choroid boundary because the sinuous nature of the peripapillary B-scan is a result of the circular-shaped acquisition line, and not natural curvature from the anatomy. This is in contrast to the linear, macular line-scans previously discussed.

To create the peripapillary grid, we first define the centre (0 degrees) of the temporal peripapillary sub-field (figure
19
(B)). This is done by cross-referencing the point along the circular acquisition region (on the en face localiser) which is co-linear with the optic disc centre and fovea centre. This position has a corresponding lateral position along the circular B-scan. If the en face localiser is unavailable, then the centre is taken as the lateral centre of the B-scan. However, without the en face localiser, the peripapillary grid will likely be off-centre which could have significant clinical consequences
[
200
]
.

Using this temporal centre, the thickness array can then be divided into temporal (-45 – 45 degrees), supero-temporal (45 – 90 degrees), supero-nasal (90 – 135 degrees), nasal (135 – -135 degrees), infero-nasal (-135 – -90 degrees) and infero-temporal (-90 – -45 degrees) sub-fields. Average thickness values are taken per sub-field, as well as the nasal-to-temporal (N/T) ratio and papillomacular bundle (PMB) (-30 – 30 degrees), with a final average over the whole B-scan. Figure
19
(C) shows these values overlaid onto the peripapillary grid, with the N/T and PMB values shown in the top-left.

{mysubsection}

[]Comparing measurements

Table
2
defines and describes the metrics used throughout this thesis to compare agreement between two sets of measurements, either choroid-derived measurements or segmentation maps outputted from a segmentation model. This section serves as a brief description of these metrics for the reader. For choroid-derived metrics, consider sets
x
and
y
to be of length
n
. For segmentation metrics, we define the
2\times 2
confusion matrix
C
which compares the agreement between two binary segmentation maps as

C=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}TP&FP\\
FN&TN\\
\end{array}\right).

(1)

Upon comparing a predicted, binary segmentation map
S_{p}
to the true segmentation map
S_{t}
, we define
TP
and
TN
as the number of true positives and negatives, i.e. the sum of all positive (1) and negative (0) matches between maps
S_{p}
and
S_{t}
, respectively.
FP
is the number of false positives, i.e. where
S_{p}
is equal to 1 but
S_{t}
is equal to 0, and vice versa for
FN
, the number of false negatives.

Name

)

)
